````
1	# Architecture
2	
3	How Half-Life's features, use cases, repositories, and data fit together.
4	
5	## Overview
6	
7	Half-Life combines The Composable Architecture (TCA) with Clean Architecture (constitution Article I). The code is split into three layers, and dependencies always point inward, toward Domain.
8	
9	| Layer | Contains | May depend on |
10	|-------|----------|---------------|
11	| Presentation | TCA features: reducers and views | Domain |
12	| Domain | Entities, business rules, single-purpose use cases, repository protocols | Nothing |
13	| Data | Repository implementations and data sources | Domain |
14	
15	Data flows in one direction:
16	
17	```
18	 Command:  View ─▶ Action ─▶ Reducer ─▶ Effect ─▶ UseCase ─▶ Repository ─▶ DataSource
19	 Update:   Repository ─▶ AsyncStream ─▶ Observe…UseCase ─▶ Action ─▶ Reducer ─▶ State ─▶ View
20	```
21	
22	Repositories are the single source of truth. A feature never writes repository-owned data into its `State` directly. It sends a command through a use case, and the new data arrives back through its observation.
23	
24	A repository can execute a Domain business rule to derive what it publishes from the data it holds. For example, it can turn caffeine intakes into a decay curve before the curve goes out on its `AsyncStream`.
25	
26	A use case performs one business operation, and that operation can act on more than one repository.
27	
28	Repositories can share a data source. A data source that stores data signals every repository subscribed to it when that data changes, and each repository re-reads what it needs and publishes it. A write through one repository reaches the others this way, so no use case writes twice just to inform another repository. For example, a drink logged through `DrinkLogRepository` reaches ``CaffeineDecayRepository`` through the drink data source they share (see <doc:DrinkComposer>).
29	
30	Only UI and presentation run on the main actor. The app target's default actor isolation is `nonisolated` (constitution Article IV.1), because TCA reducers crash under a `MainActor` default, and marking TCA types `nonisolated` under that default leaves compiler warnings. Every UI and presentation type states its isolation explicitly, and SwiftLint enforces it.
31	
32	- **UI types are `@MainActor`.** Views, the app, and other UI types have `@MainActor` on the line directly above the declaration, even where `View` or `App` would infer it. TCA's `Store` is `@MainActor`.
33	- **Reducers are `nonisolated`.** Each `@Reducer` type is written `@Reducer nonisolated struct …`, and is never `@MainActor`, because that crashes at runtime. A reducer reduces `Sendable` state, and the store runs it on the main actor.
34	- **Domain types are immutable `Sendable` values.** Each actor works on its own copy, so there's no shared mutable state to protect. Business rules and use cases hold no state. Domain structs written before the default changed still say `nonisolated`, which is redundant but harmless.
35	- **Protocols never say `nonisolated`.** In the same module, an actor that conforms to a `nonisolated` protocol inherits `nonisolated`, which an actor can't be, so the module fails to build. The error only appears once the protocol also has a second conformer in the module, such as a test double in a dependency registration. Under the target's default, the keyword adds nothing to a protocol anyway.
36	- **Repositories are their own actors** (Article I.13). A repository executes business rules on its own actor and hands entities to presentation.
37	
38	### Lifetimes
39	
40	| Kind | Lifetime | Holds state? |
````

````
1	# Language Model
2	
3	How Half-Life asks Apple's on-device language model for text, the tools the model can call, and when the features that use it are hidden.
4	
5	## Overview
6	
7	The Foundation Models layer (roadmap rank 11) gives other features one way to ask Apple's on-device language model for text. Three roadmap items build on it: insight cards (rank 12), App Intents (rank 14), which carry the brief's "text the app" bonus, and the Insights tab (rank 15). None of them is built yet, so nothing on screen shows the model's text yet.
8	
9	**The rules calculate, and the model only phrases.** The model never works out a number. Every amount, time, and drink it mentions comes from a tool, and each tool reads it from the same repository the screens read. An answer can't disagree with the Today screen.
10	
11	### Decisions
12	
13	The owner settled these on 2026-09-12.
14	
15	| Decision | Why |
16	|----------|-----|
17	| Tools read repositories, never data sources. | Constitution Article V.3.5 lets only repositories reach HealthKit data sources, and Article I.10 has repositories execute the business rules. A tool that read a data source would have to re-run the rules, and could disagree with the screen. |
18	| Tools only read, except `logDrink`, which logs a drink through ``LogDrinkUseCase``. | Logging goes through the same use case as the composer, one-tap favourites, and App Intents, so it gets the same checks. |
19	| Only instructions from App Intents get `logDrink`. | The model can log a drink only when the user asked for one, in their own words. Text the app writes, such as a request for an insight, can never log a drink. |
20	| Each session handles a single instruction. | Half-Life isn't a chatbot. Each instruction gets a new session and new tools, which are discarded with its response. Nothing carries from one instruction to the next, and nothing is persisted. |
21	| Features that use the model are hidden while it's unavailable. | The app works fully without Apple Intelligence, as it does without Health (Article V.3.3). |
22	| The data source is `LanguageModelDataSource`, implemented by `FoundationModelLanguageModelDataSource`. | The owner chose the singular, although the framework is `FoundationModels`. The files live in `Data/DataSources/FoundationModels/`, named for the framework. |
23	
24	## How an instruction runs
25	
26	```
27	Feature or App Intent ─▶ RespondToInstructionUseCase ─▶ LanguageModelRepository.respond(to:)
28	  ─▶ LanguageModelDataSource.respond(to:)
29	       ─▶ a new LanguageModelSession(tools:instructions:) ─▶ one response, then discarded
30	               │
31	               ├─▶ read-only tools ─▶ CaffeineDecayRepository, DrinkLogRepository, CurrentTimeRepository
32	               └─▶ logDrink (App Intents only) ─▶ LogDrinkUseCase ─▶ DrinkLogRepository
33	```
34	
35	- **The response is returned, not published.** The repository doesn't keep it, so it isn't repository-owned data, and returning it isn't an exception to constitution Article I.5's one-way flow. A drink that `logDrink` stores does flow the usual way: the drink log data source signals the change, and the Today screen, the curve, and the favourites follow.
36	- **The tools are part of the data source, and they read repositories.** A tool conforms to the framework's `Tool` protocol, and only data sources import `FoundationModels` (Article I.14). Tools read the Domain repository protocols, never another data source. This is the only data source that reads repositories.
37	- **Each instruction builds its own tools**, so the ``LogDrinkUseCase`` that `logDrink` holds lives for a single instruction (Article I.8).
38	
39	```
40	Half-Life/Data/DataSources/FoundationModels/
````

1	# One-Tap Log
2	
3	The user's three favourite drinks, each logged in one tap on the Today screen and in the drink composer.
4	
5	## Overview
6	
7	The brief's first requirement is "Log a drink in one tap." One-tap favourites are roadmap rank 3. A row of three buttons shows the drinks the user logs most. One tap logs that drink, at that quantity, as consumed now. There's no confirmation step, so the button confirms the log after it's saved.
8	
9	The row appears in two places:
10	
11	- **The Today screen**, under the decay card and its tiles, headed "One tap". Each button shows the drink's icon, name, quantity, and caffeine.
12	- **The drink composer**, under its header, in a slim style that shows only the name, quantity, and caffeine. A tap logs the drink and closes the composer, as Add does (<doc:DrinkComposer>).
13	
14	It's built from these pieces:
15	
16	- ``FavouriteDrink`` and ``FavouriteDrinksRule``, in the Domain.
17	- ``FavouriteDrinksRepository``, implemented by ``LiveFavouriteDrinksRepository`` over the shared ``DrinkLogDataSource``.
18	- ``ObserveFavouriteDrinksUseCase``, and the existing ``LogDrinkUseCase``.
19	- ``OneTapLogFeature`` and ``OneTapLogView``, which ``TodayFeature`` and ``DrinkComposerFeature`` each compose.
20	
21	## What the prototype shows
22	
23	| Prototype element (screenshots 07 and 10) | Becomes |
24	|-------------------------------------------|---------|
25	| "ONE TAP": Double espresso 128 mg, Oat flat white 64 mg, Cold brew 205 mg | The three favourites the user logs most. Until the log has three, starters fill the row: 2 espresso shots, 2 flat white shots, and 2 cups of cold brew (125, 125, and 205 mg). |
26	| "Double espresso", "Oat flat white" | The drink's name and quantity, such as "Espresso, 2 shots". The catalog has no double espresso, and milk is cut (<doc:DrinkComposer>). |
27	| The letter in each tile (E, F, C) | The drink's SF Symbol, the one the composer's tiles use, in a `dataCaffeineSubtle` icon tile |
28	| "All drinks" | Cut. The tab bar's "Log a drink" tab opens the composer, which lists every drink. |
29	| The composer's "Tap a favourite, or build it below." and "128 mg · one tap" | The composer's slim row, with no heading or subtitle, so the sheet stays compact |
30	
31	## Decisions
32	
33	The owner decided these on 2026-09-12:
34	
35	| Question | Decision | Rejected |
36	|----------|----------|----------|
37	| What counts as one favourite | A drink and its quantity together. "Latte, 2 shots" and "Latte, 3 shots" are different favourites, so each button logs exactly what the user usually has. | The drink alone, at the quantity it's most often logged at. Two sizes of one drink couldn't both show. |
38	| Over what period | Every drink ever logged. The favourites change only when the log does, so nothing recalculates as days pass. | The last 30 days. That would follow changing habits, but the repository would have to recalculate as days pass, and the row would empty after a month away. |
39	| Ties | The favourite consumed most recently ranks first. | Catalog order |
40	| Fewer than three favourites | The starters fill the empty slots. The row is titled "One tap", not "Your favourites", so it doesn't claim to know the user's habits before it does (the brief's *Honesty* criterion). | Showing only what's been logged, and hiding the row until something has |
```

### 2026-09-12 22:13 -0400 — Design the personal half-life estimator

- **Started:** 2026-09-12 22:13 -0400
- **Ended:** 2026-09-12 22:17 -0400
- **Human:** Adam Ure
- **AI tool / model:** Claude Code / Claude Opus 5
- **Transcript:** `ai_transcripts/2026-09-12-2213-cc37a791.md`
- **Type:** planning, question
- **Request:** Design the architecture for the personal half-life estimator (roadmap rank 9). Explain what's known about the body's response to caffeine, and whether sleep, steps, and resting heart rate from Apple Health, plus the onboarding survey, can estimate the user's half-life.
- **Interactions:**
  - `22:13` The owner asked for the estimator's architecture and for the physiology behind it.
- **AI contribution:**
  - **Read the existing code first.** `HalfLifePriorRule` already turns onboarding's factors into a starting half-life, `CaffeineDecayRule` models each drink with the Bateman function, and the sleep, step count, and resting heart rate data sources exist with no repository reading them. The decay repository reads the half-life through `HalfLifeDataSource`, which `FileProfileDataSource` implements.
  - **Physiology.** Half-life is set by the liver enzyme CYP1A2. Health data can't measure it. Sleep carries some information about it, but only indirectly, as an effect. Steps and resting heart rate carry almost none, and are more useful as covariates that explain nights that were bad for other reasons. How strongly a given caffeine level disturbs sleep (adenosine receptor sensitivity) varies separately from how fast it's cleared.
  - **Identifiability.** A long half-life with low sensitivity looks like a short one with high sensitivity. Only variety in when the user drinks separates them, and nightly sleep is noisy. The AI's back-of-envelope estimate is that a month of real Apple Watch data often won't move the estimate far from the survey's prior. That estimate hasn't been checked, and the demo seed generator (rank 8) is the way to check it.
...
</persisted-output>

//  _______  __   __  ___   ___      ___      _______  __    _  _______
// |       ||  | |  ||   | |   |    |   |    |   _   ||  |  | ||       |

// Half-Life LogDrinkUseCase
//

import Foundation

/// Records a drink the user consumed.
///
/// The composer, one-tap favourites, and App Intents all log drinks through this use case. It reads the current
/// time, builds the drink, and logs it through ``DrinkLogRepository``, which checks it with ``DrinkLogRule``. The
/// decay curve hears about the drink from the data source the two repositories share, not from this use case.
struct LogDrinkUseCase: UseCase {
    /// The drink to record.
    struct Input: Sendable, Equatable {
        /// Which drink.
        let type: DrinkType
        /// How many units of the drink's ``DrinkType/unit``.
        let quantity: Int
        /// How long before now the drink was consumed, in seconds. 0 is now.
        let secondsAgo: TimeInterval
    }

    /// The repository that supplies the current time.
    let currentTime: any CurrentTimeRepository
    /// The repository that stores the drink.
    let drinkLog: any DrinkLogRepository

    /// Logs the drink consumed `input.secondsAgo` seconds before now, with its estimated caffeine.
    ///
    /// - Parameter input: The drink to record.
    /// - Throws: The repository's error if the drink can't be logged or stored.
    func execute(_ input: Input) async throws {

- **AI contribution:**
  - **Read first.** The AI read the Architecture article, the repository and data source protocols, the key entities, the roadmap, and the half-life estimator design from 22:13. It checked the `Tool` protocol, `SystemLanguageModel.Availability`, `contextSize`, `tokenCount(for:)`, and `GenerationError` in the iOS 26.5 SDK's `FoundationModels.swiftinterface`.
  - **Raised a conflict with the constitution.** Having tools read data sources directly conflicts with Article V.3.5, which says each HealthKit data source is reached only through repositories. It also conflicts with Article I.10, which says repositories execute business rules. The tools would have to re-run `CaffeineDecayRule` and the other rules, so the model's numbers could drift from the ones on screen. The AI recommended that the tools read the Domain repository protocols instead. Tools reading data sources directly would need both articles amended.
  - **Drafted the architecture.** A `LanguageModelDataSource` protocol and a `FoundationModelsLanguageModelDataSource` implementation, in `Data/DataSources/FoundationModels/` with `Tools/` and `Generable/` subfolders. `LiveAssistantRepository` would sit in front of it. The draft's principles:
    - Rules calculate and the model only phrases.
    - Tools return compact, pre-formatted facts with how much data backs them, because the context window is small.
    - Tools only read. Drinks are logged through guided generation and `LogDrinkUseCase`.
    - The app works fully when Apple Intelligence is unavailable.
    - Prompts, answers, and tool input and output are never logged.
    - The conversation is held in memory only.
  - **Brainstormed tools.** 16 candidates, each marked by whether its repository exists today, needs a small addition, or waits on a later roadmap item (ranks 7, 9, 12, 15). Drink parsing and insight copy were kept as guided generation, not tools.
  - **Revised the draft after the owner's decisions.**
    - Each instruction gets a fresh session and fresh tools, which are discarded after the one response. The use case the log tool holds therefore lives for a single instruction, which keeps Article I.8. With no transcript, the repository holds only the availability.
    - The AI checked the SDK: `SystemLanguageModel` is `Sendable` but not `Observable`, so nothing reports an availability change. The data source re-reads availability when the app becomes active.
    - The log tool's arguments mirror `LogDrinkUseCase.Input`. The drink is a `@Generable` enum that mirrors `DrinkType`, because Domain can't import FoundationModels. The tool returns what was logged, and reports a `DrinkLogRule` violation back to the model instead of throwing.
    - Renamed `LiveAssistantRepository` to `LiveLanguageModelRepository`, because the app isn't a chatbot.
  - **Flagged:**
    - The owner's `FoundationModel…` is singular. The framework is `FoundationModels`, and the naming convention (`<Framework><Noun>DataSource`) gives the plural.
    - The Insights design (22:43) has tonight's window fall back to a template sentence. Under the owner's rule, that sentence is hidden.
- **Human changes:** The owner decided that tools read the repositories, that they're read-only except for one log tool, the names, that model features are hidden when the model is unavailable, and that sessions are single-instruction and never persisted. The AI's recommendations were template copy when the model is unavailable and read-only tools with no log tool. The owner rejected both. The owner kept the singular name over the framework's plural, and limited the log tool to App Intents instructions.
- **Files:** `ai_log.md`

import ComposableArchitecture
import Foundation

extension DependencyValues {
    /// The app's drink log repository (constitution Article I.15).
    ///
    /// Live, it's one app-scoped ``LiveDrinkLogRepository`` over the shared drink log data source, which the caffeine
    /// decay repository reads too. In previews, it's the same repository over an empty in-memory store. In tests,
    /// using it without overriding it reports an issue.
    var drinkLogRepository: any DrinkLogRepository {
        get { self[DrinkLogRepositoryKey.self] }
        set { self[DrinkLogRepositoryKey.self] = newValue }
    }

    /// Logs a drink through the app-scoped ``drinkLogRepository``, with the time from ``currentTimeRepository``.
    ///
    /// In tests, using it without overriding it reports an issue.
    var logDrink: LogDrinkUseCase {
        get { self[LogDrinkUseCaseKey.self] }
        set { self[LogDrinkUseCaseKey.self] = newValue }
    }

    /// Observes every logged drink through the app-scoped ``drinkLogRepository``.
    ///
    /// In tests, using it without overriding it reports an issue.
    var observeLoggedDrinks: ObserveLoggedDrinksUseCase {
        get { self[ObserveLoggedDrinksUseCaseKey.self] }
        set { self[ObserveLoggedDrinksUseCaseKey.self] = newValue }
    }

    /// Observes the caffeine logged today through the app-scoped ``drinkLogRepository``.
    ///
    /// In tests, using it without overriding it reports an issue.
    var observeCaffeineIntakeToday: ObserveCaffeineIntakeTodayUseCase {
        get { self[ObserveCaffeineIntakeTodayUseCaseKey.self] }
        set { self[ObserveCaffeineIntakeTodayUseCaseKey.self] = newValue }
    }

    /// Observes one day of the drink log through the app-scoped ``drinkLogRepository``.
    ///

I found comprehensive information about App Intents interactive snippets in iOS 26. Here are the key results:

## Overview
Apple introduced interactive snippets with App Intents in iOS 26 at WWDC25, enabling a new level of interactivity for apps outside of their main interface. Users can now interact with app features from system-level experiences like Siri and the Shortcuts app, or even directly from Spotlight on a Mac, without needing to open the app itself.

## SnippetIntent Protocol
SnippetIntent is an app intent that presents an interactive snippet onscreen. iOS 26 introduces the dedicated SnippetIntent protocol for snippet-shaped interactions, alongside the existing ShowsSnippetView-conforming AppIntent pattern from earlier releases.

## Key Features
To implement a snippet intent, it needs to conform to the SnippetIntent protocol and include the variables it needs to render the view correctly. The supportedModes property can be set to foreground to open the app before the intent is performed.

## Interactive Capabilities
The requestConfirmation(actionName:snippetIntent:) method is a new method introduced in iOS 26 that can be invoked within the body of any perform() method of an app intent and is asynchronous, waiting until the user completes the confirmation action.

For detailed official documentation, Apple's official documentation covers "Displaying static and interactive snippets."


REMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown hyperlinks.

# AppDependencyManager Summary

## What It Does
**AppDependencyManager** is "an object that manages the registration and initialization of an app intent's dependencies."

## API

### Type Property
- **`shared`**: A static singleton instance for app-wide dependency registration

### Instance Methods (Three Overloads of `add`)
1. **`add(key:dependency:)`** - Synchronous dependency factory
2. **`add(key:dependency:)`** - Synchronous throwing dependency factory  
3. **`add(key:dependency:)`** - Async throwing dependency factory

All variants accept an optional `AnyHashable` key and an `@autoclosure` or closure that produces the dependency.

### Initializer
- **`init()`** - "Can be used to initialize a standalone `AppDependencyManager` for dependency injection during testing."

## Property Wrapper
Dependencies are resolved using the **`@AppDependency`** property wrapper, which is "a property wrapper that resolves a registered dependency at runtime."

## Availability
- **iOS 16.0+**
- **iPadOS 16.0+**
- **macOS 13.0+**
- **tvOS 16.0+**
- **watchOS 9.0+**
- **visionOS** (all versions)

## Documentation Path
`/documentation/appintents/appdependencymanager`
```

# AppIntentsTesting Framework Summary

## What It Is
The AppIntentsTesting framework enables testing of app intents, entities, and queries. As stated in the documentation:

> "Test your app intents, entities, queries, and integration with system features like Siri or Spotlight."

It allows developers to "run and test your app intents, entities, enums, and query logic **out-of-process** — the same way Siri or Shortcuts perform them."

## Availability
- **Introduced in version 27.0** across all major platforms:
  - iOS, iPadOS, Mac Catalyst, macOS, tvOS, visionOS, watchOS

## Main Types

**Type-Erased Intermediate Representations:**
- `AnyAppIntent` - type-erased app intent for testing
- `AnyAppEntity` - type-erased app entity
- `AnyEntityQuery` - type-erased entity query
- `AnyAppEnum` - type-erased enumeration with dynamic value access
- `AnyTransientAppEntity` - type-erased transient entity with dynamic property access

**Result Verification:**
- `ResolvedIntentResult` - type-safe result from performing an intent
- `ResolvedValueQueryResult` - result of intent value queries

**Supporting Types:**
- `IntentDefinitions` - catalog of app intents, enums, entities, and queries
- `DynamicPropertyPath` - type-safe dynamic path to nested intent values
- `ViewAnnotation` - onscreen context for entity annotations

## Key Articles
- `/documentation/appintentstesting/testing-your-app-intents-code` - Essential guide for evaluating intents and verifying system integration

## In-Process vs Out-of-Process
**Out-of-process** - The framework provides "type-erased APIs that let you reference intents by name, set their parameters, and run them **without linking against your app target**," matching how system features execute them.

## Swift Testing Compatibility
*Not explicitly documented in provided content.*
```

```
| `unit` | `ServingUnit` | What the quantity counts: shots, cups, or cans. |
| `milligramsPerUnit` | `Double` | The estimated caffeine in one unit. Always greater than zero. |
| `defaultQuantity` | `Int` | The quantity the composer starts at. At least 1. |

It has one operation: `estimatedMilligrams(quantity:)`, which returns `milligramsPerUnit × quantity`.

A drink's type is an enum rather than data loaded at runtime, because the list is fixed and short, and App Intents needs it at compile time. An App Intent parameter that offers a fixed list is an `AppEnum`, whose cases and display names are static. If users could ever add their own drinks, the catalog would move into a repository.

Once shipped, cases are never removed and raw values never change. Stored drinks refer to them.

### ServingUnit

What a drink's quantity counts.
1. It reads the current time from ``CurrentTimeRepository``.
2. It creates the `LoggedDrink`, with a new `UUID`, `milligrams` from `type.estimatedMilligrams(quantity:)`, and `consumedAt` set to the current time minus `secondsAgo`.
3. It calls `log(_:)`, and passes on any error the repository throws.

It doesn't check the drink itself, because the repository's rule does. It doesn't tell the decay curve about the drink either. The curve hears about it from the data source (see "Data flow").

### Entry points

| Entry point | Drink type | Quantity | `secondsAgo` |
|-------------|------------|----------|--------------|
| Composer (rank 2) | The row picked from the list | Starts at the drink's `defaultQuantity`, and a stepper adjusts it | 0, or the "When" choice: 3,600, 7,200, or 14,400 |
| One-tap favourite (rank 3) | One of the three favourites | The favourite's own: a favourite is a drink and a quantity together | 0 |
| App Intent or Siri (rank 14) | An `AppEnum` parameter that mirrors `DrinkType` | Optional, defaulting to the drink's `defaultQuantity` | 0 |

An App Intent calls the use case directly, without a reducer. How its `@Dependency` reaches the same app-scoped repository is designed with App Intents.

## Current time

The use case and the drink log's rule both need the current time. The owner decided on 2026-09-11 that one repository supplies it for the whole app. `CurrentTimeRepository` replaced the Today screen's planned `TimeOfDayRepository`, and `ObserveTimeOfDayUseCase` finds each minute's day period from its stream.
## Privacy and logging

A logged drink is health data. Its type, quantity, amount, and time all come down to a caffeine intake.

- It's stored once, by the drink data source, in a SwiftData store on the device. The store syncs to the user's own private CloudKit database, which the developer can't read (constitution Article V.1). The same record carries the intake's negligible mark. The Architecture article's "Data and privacy" table lists it.
- The sync is configured, and the rest of the CloudKit work belongs to CloudKit backup (roadmap rank 23): confirming the container in the developer account, deploying the schema to Production, push-driven sync, testing on a device signed in to iCloud, and the App Store privacy label.
- The store keeps iOS's default protection class, complete until first unlock (`NSFileProtectionCompleteUntilFirstUserAuthentication`). The owner chose it on 2026-09-11, as the weaker class that Article V.4 allows for background access. Once the device has been unlocked since it started up, CloudKit imports and Siri can read the store while the device is locked. Before that first unlock, the store is unreadable.
- None of its fields is ever logged, at any level (Article XI.6.1). A successful log is a routine health event, so it's logged at `debug` at most. A failed one is logged at `error`, with the error's domain and code and no values (Article XI.7).
```

# Summary of App Intent Runtime Configuration

Based on the provided article content, here are the findings for your specific queries:

## authenticationPolicy / Locked Device Behavior
**Not covered.** The article does not mention `IntentAuthenticationPolicy`, authentication policies, or behavior on locked devices.

## supportedModes / IntentModes
**Covered.** The article discusses this extensively:

> "When it's time to run an app intent, the system consults the app intent's `supportedModes` property to determine whether to run it in the foreground or background."

The article explains that `supportedModes` allows "nuanced runtime behaviors" including the ability to "tell the system you want the app intent to run in the background initially but transition your app to the foreground at some point later."

**Key constraint:** "The information in your app intent's `supportedModes` property is only a suggestion to the system. The location of your app intent code, the type of your app intent, and current system conditions also influence the system's choice."

## openAppWhenRun Deprecation
**Not covered.** No mention of `openAppWhenRun` or deprecation notices.

## Where perform() Runs
**Covered indirectly.** The article states:

> "The system runs this method after bringing your app to the foreground, so animate any changes you make for a smoother transition. Make all interface-related changes from your app's main thread using a `MainActor` type or other approach."

This implies `perform()` executes in the app process when running in foreground mode.

**Not covered.** The article does not discuss data protection or background app launching mechanisms.

# App Intents Snippets Summary

## Result Snippet vs. Interactive SnippetIntent

**Result Snippet (ShowsSnippetView/ShowsSnippetIntent):**
- Returns a static view from `perform()`: `return .result(view: Text("Some example text.").font(.title))`
- Displays outcome without follow-up actions
- Simple, information-only presentation

**Interactive SnippetIntent:**
- An app intent that "conforms to `SnippetIntent`" and handles "snippet's layout and interactive components"
- The primary intent returns `ShowsSnippetIntent & ReturnsValue<Entity>`, which references a `SnippetIntent`
- Enables "people to perform an action from a snippet instead of viewing static information"
- Example: landmark snippet includes "a button to add it to a list favorites, and a button to search for available tickets"

## Button and Toggle in Snippets

**Requirement:** "Like widgets, you must initialize the snippet's `Button` or `Toggle` with an `AppIntent` that performs the underlying action."

**Lifecycle:** When a person taps an interactive control, "the system performs the [corresponding intent] to make the change. It discards the snippet's old SwiftUI views, and performs the `SnippetIntent` again to provide a new version of the snippet."

## Size/Height Limits & Design Guidance

**No explicit limits provided in this document.** The guidance emphasizes:
- "Snippets are great candidates to reuse views you use in your widgets"
- Keep snippets responsive by "Avoiding long-running tasks to ensure the snippet appears responsive"

## Snippet Rendering Process

**Not explicitly stated.** The document notes: "As long as a snippet is visible, the system keeps your app active in the background, retaining all information in memory."

## Dialog and Snippet Combination

The primary intent can return both simultaneously:
```swift
return .result(
    value: landmark,
    dialog: IntentDialog(
        full: "The closest landmark is \(landmark.name).",
        supporting: "\(landmark.name) is located in \(landmark.continent)."
````

## Key Findings on How Siri with Apple Intelligence Uses App Intents

### Core Mechanism
**Quote:** *"Apple Intelligence combines language models with your app's actions and content to power Siri AI. It uses the app intents, app entities, and app enums you define to wrap your app's actions and content in types the system can understand."*

### Five Essential Steps

1. **Indexing (Content Discovery)**
   - **Quote:** *"Index entities to make them available in Spotlight. Apple Intelligence uses the semantic search capabilities of Spotlight to find your app's content, even when someone describes it vaguely."*

2. **Transferable Types (Cross-App Functionality)**
   - **Quote:** *"Conforming your AppEntity to Transferable or types from the App Intents framework enables the system to move content across apps so people can perform tasks across apps with Siri AI."*

# IntentAuthenticationPolicy Cases

Based on the Apple App Intents documentation, here are all cases of `IntentAuthenticationPolicy`:

## Cases

1. **`alwaysAllowed`**
   - Description: "A policy that allows the app intent to run at any time, including when the device is locked."
   - Documentation path: `/documentation/appintents/intentauthenticationpolicy/alwaysallowed`

2. **`requiresAuthentication`**
   - Description: "A policy that requires authentication before running the app intent."
   - Documentation path: `/documentation/appintents/intentauthenticationpolicy/requiresauthentication`

3. **`requiresLocalDeviceAuthentication`**
   - Description: "A policy that requires the person to unlock the device running the intent."
   - Documentation path: `/documentation/appintents/intentauthenticationpolicy/requireslocaldeviceauthentication`

## Locked Device References

Only `alwaysAllowed` explicitly mentions locked device behavior: **"including when the device is locked."**

The `requiresLocalDeviceAuthentication` case references unlocking but describes it as a requirement rather than permission to run while locked.

Based on the search results, here's what I found about localizing App Shortcuts phrases using AppShortcuts.xcstrings:

## Key Information About AppShortcuts.xcstrings

You can localize your App shortcuts using the newer Strings Catalog format (.xcstrings), with the file name AppShortcuts.xcstrings, which automatically extracts localized content from your app during the build.

## The applicationName Parameter

Every App Shortcut utterance should have '${applicationName}' in it. This parameter is a required element for App Shortcuts phrases, and phrases without variables should only have ${applicationName} appear in them for certain display scenarios in the Shortcuts library.

## File Structure for Localization

AppShortcuts.strings (or the xcstrings equivalent) is specifically for all the AppShortcut/AppShortcutsProvider phrases, and it has to be called exactly like this.

## Phrase Requirements

A localization that contains multiple instances of the same parameter would cause failures—for example: "Select ${model} in ${applicationName} for ${model}" has ${model} twice in the same string.

The search results primarily reference developer discussions rather than official Apple documentation on developer.apple.com, but they provide practical guidance from developers working with App Shortcuts localization.


REMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown hyperlinks.

# AppEnum Summary

## Purpose
`AppEnum` is a protocol that "express[es] that a custom type has a predefined, static set of values." It enables enumerations to be used in app intents and entities by adding metadata required by Siri and system features.

## Protocol Requirements

### 1. **typeDisplayRepresentation**
A static property providing "A short, localized, human-readable name for the type."

### 2. **caseDisplayRepresentations**
A static dictionary that maps "each value to the visual elements that represent it."

## Compile-Time Metadata Rule
The documentation states these must be **static constant values**, though it doesn't explicitly use the phrase "compile-time metadata extraction." However, the example shows they are implemented as `static var` properties, implying they must be evaluable at compile time for Siri and system integration.

## Extension Conformance
The documentation does **not explicitly address** whether conformance can be declared in an extension. Standard Swift protocol rules would apply—conformance could typically be declared in an extension, but the synthesized/default implementations of display representations may need to be in the primary type.

## Code Example
```swift
enum ActivityStyle: String, AppEnum {
    case biking, equestrian, hiking, jogging, crossCountrySkiing, snowshoeing
    
    static var typeDisplayRepresentation: TypeDisplayRepresentation {
        TypeDisplayRepresentation(
            name: LocalizedStringResource("Activity style", table: "AppIntents"),
            numericFormat: LocalizedStringResource("\\(placeholder: .int) data", table: "AppIntents"))
    }
    
    static var caseDisplayRepresentations: [Self: DisplayRepresentation] = [
        .biking: DisplayRepresentation(title: "Biking", subtitle: "Mountain bike ride"),
        // ... more cases
    ]
}
```

## Documentation Path
`doc://com.apple.AppIntents/documentation/AppIntents/AppEnum`
````

# App Intents

How Siri, Shortcuts, Spotlight, and the Action Button log drinks and answer questions about caffeine, through the same use cases the screens use.

## Overview

App Intents are roadmap rank 14. They let the user log a drink and ask about their caffeine without opening the app: by voice, by typing to Siri, from a shortcut, or with the Action Button. They also carry the brief's "text the app" bonus. The roadmap puts that bonus "on a better substrate": typing to Siri stands in for texting a number, which would need a server that constitution Article V.1 rules out.

**Status: design, not built.** This article starts with the architecture. The decisions still open are listed at the end, and testable requirements will follow once they're settled.

**The intents add no business logic.** Each intent is a thin entry point. It calls a use case that already exists, and phrases what comes back. Every number Siri says comes from the same repository and business rule as the number on the Today screen, so the two can't disagree.

## Architecture

```
 Siri, spoken or typed · Shortcuts · Spotlight · Action Button
                │  an App Shortcut phrase, or an intent run with its parameters
                ▼
 ┌─ Presentation ── Half-Life/AppIntents/ ── imports AppIntents, runs in the app's own process
 │
 │   HalfLifeShortcuts: AppShortcutsProvider ─── phrases, from AppShortcuts.xcstrings
 │
 │   LogDrinkIntent ────────────┐   drink: DrinkTypeAppEnum, quantity: Int?
 │   GetCaffeineStatusIntent ───┤
 │   GetLastCupIntent ──────────┤   perform() reads a use case with @Dependencies.Dependency,
 │   GetCaffeineTodayIntent ────┤   and returns an IntentDialog
 │   AskHalfLifeIntent ─────────┤   question: String
 │                              │
 └──────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
                                ▼
 ┌─ Domain ── existing use cases, the same app-scoped instances the reducers get
 │
 │   LogDrinkUseCase                    ◀── LogDrinkIntent
 │   ObserveCaffeineStatusUseCase       ◀── GetCaffeineStatusIntent   takes the first value
 │   ObserveCaffeineCutoffUseCase       ◀── GetLastCupIntent          takes the first value
 │   ObserveCaffeineIntakeTodayUseCase  ◀── GetCaffeineTodayIntent    takes the first value
 │   RespondToInstructionUseCase        ◀── AskHalfLifeIntent         origin: .appIntent
 │
 └──────────────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
                                ▼
 ┌─ Data ── app-scoped repositories and data sources, unchanged
 │
 │   DrinkLogRepository ─────────┬─▶ DrinkLogDataSource (SwiftData) ── signals each change ──▶
 │   CaffeineDecayRepository ────┘     every repository re-reads, and open screens update
 │   CaffeineDecayRepository ──────▶ FileProfileDataSource (bedtime), EstimatedHalfLifeDataSource
 │   CurrentTimeRepository ────────▶ ClockDataSource
 │   LanguageModelRepository ──────▶ FoundationModelLanguageModelDataSource
 │                                     its tools read the repositories above,
 │                                     and its logDrink tool calls LogDrinkUseCase
 └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
```

The three kinds of request, end to end:

```
 Log:    "Log a latte in Half-Life" ─▶ LogDrinkIntent ─▶ LogDrinkUseCase ─▶ DrinkLogRepository.log (DrinkLogRule)
           ─▶ DrinkLogDataSource.store ─▶ signal ─▶ each repository re-reads ─▶ IntentDialog: what was logged

 Query:  "How much caffeine is in me, Half-Life?" ─▶ GetCaffeineStatusIntent ─▶ ObserveCaffeineStatusUseCase
           ─▶ the first CaffeineStatus the stream sends ─▶ IntentDialog: the level now, and at bedtime

 Ask:    "Ask Half-Life" ─▶ Siri asks "What would you like to know?" ─▶ AskHalfLifeIntent
           ─▶ RespondToInstructionUseCase ─▶ LanguageModelRepository ─▶ one session, with the read-only tools
              and logDrink ─▶ the model's answer ─▶ IntentDialog
```

### Where the intents sit

- **Intents are Presentation.** Like reducers, they depend only on Domain use cases, never on a repository or a data source (constitution Article I.3). `AppIntents` is a presentation framework, like SwiftUI, so importing it doesn't make an intent a data source. Domain never imports it.
- **Intents aren't reducers.** An intent has no screen and no state that outlives it. It performs one command or answers one question, and it's gone. A TCA store would add nothing, so each intent calls its use case directly, as the Drink Composer article already records. The constitution doesn't cover presentation that isn't a reducer, so this needs the owner's decision (see "Decisions to make").
- **Reads take the first value.** Each `Observe…` use case's stream sends the current value as soon as it's subscribed to (Article I.12). An intent reads that first value and stops, which ends the subscription. There's no new use case and no new repository operation. The Language Model article's tools read the same way. The rejected alternative was a `Get…UseCase` for each query, which would duplicate each `Observe…` use case and need a one-shot operation on every repository.
- **Writes flow the usual way.** A drink that ``LogDrinkUseCase`` stores reaches the curve, the totals, and the favourites through the drink log data source they share, as it does from the composer. No intent writes to `State`.
- **The intents run in the app's process, not an extension.** Intents in the app target run in the app, which the system launches in the background when it isn't running. So they reach the same app-scoped repositories as the screens. An App Intents extension would be a second process, with its own repositories over the same SwiftData store, meaning two writers and signals that don't cross between them. Each intent's `supportedModes` is `.background`, so nothing opens the app.

### How an intent reaches its use case

Intents get their use cases from swift-dependencies, through the same `DependencyValues` keys that reducers use (constitution Article I.15). A live intent therefore gets the one app-scoped repository behind each use case. This answers the question the Drink Composer article left for this design.

There's one pitfall, found in the iOS 26.5 SDK's `AppIntents.swiftinterface`. `AppIntent` conforms to `_SupportsAppDependencies`, and an extension on that protocol declares `typealias Dependency = AppIntents.AppDependency`. Inside an intent, a bare `@Dependency(\.logDrink)` therefore resolves to Apple's `AppDependency`, not swift-dependencies' property wrapper. Intents write the module name:

```swift
/// Logs a drink the user asked Siri or a shortcut to log.
nonisolated struct LogDrinkIntent: AppIntent {
    static let title: LocalizedStringResource = "Log a Drink"
    static let supportedModes: IntentModes = .background

    @Parameter(title: "Drink") var drink: DrinkTypeAppEnum
    @Parameter(title: "Quantity") var quantity: Int?

    @Dependencies.Dependency(\.logDrink) private var logDrink

    func perform() async throws -> some IntentResult & ProvidesDialog {
        // Call the use case, then return the dialog that a separately tested function builds.
    }
}
```

Whether the `Dependencies` module name resolves through TCA's re-export, or needs its own `import Dependencies`, is checked at the first build.

The rejected alternative is Apple's `AppDependencyManager`, with `@AppDependency`. It's first-party (Article III.2), but it's a second registry with no test or preview values, and it could hold a different repository than the store does. Article I.15 names swift-dependencies.

### Isolation

- **Intents and the shortcuts provider are `nonisolated`, written out** (constitution Article IV.1). Their `perform()` awaits `Sendable` use cases and actor repositories, and touches no UI, so nothing needs the main actor.
- **`DrinkTypeAppEnum` is a `nonisolated` enum.**
- **A snippet view, if the owner chooses one, is `@MainActor`**, like every view. The existing SwiftLint rule already covers it. A new rule for intents is one of the decisions below.

## Folder structure

```
Half-Life/AppIntents/
├── HalfLifeShortcuts.swift          AppShortcutsProvider: each shortcut's intent, phrases, title, and symbol
├── DrinkTypeAppEnum.swift           An AppEnum mirror of DrinkType
├── IntentDialogFormat.swift         Builds each dialog from Domain values. Pure, and unit-tested.
├── LogDrinkIntent.swift
├── GetCaffeineStatusIntent.swift
├── GetLastCupIntent.swift
├── GetCaffeineTodayIntent.swift
└── AskHalfLifeIntent.swift
Half-Life/AppShortcuts.xcstrings     The App Shortcut phrases, in the catalog App Intents requires
```

## Intents

The phrases are English drafts. Every phrase includes the app's name, `\(.applicationName)`, which App Shortcuts require.

| Intent | Phrases | Parameters | Use case | Answer |
|--------|---------|------------|----------|--------|
| `LogDrinkIntent` | "Log a \(\.$drink) in \(.applicationName)", "Log a drink in \(.applicationName)" | `drink`, required, so Siri asks for it when the phrase doesn't name one. `quantity`, optional, defaulting to the drink's `defaultQuantity`. | ``LogDrinkUseCase``, consumed now (`secondsAgo` 0) | "Logged a latte, 2 shots: about 125 mg." Or why it wasn't logged: ``DrinkLogRule``'s violation, in words. |
| `GetCaffeineStatusIntent` | "How much caffeine is in me, \(.applicationName)?", "What's my caffeine level in \(.applicationName)?" | None | ``ObserveCaffeineStatusUseCase`` | "About 85 mg now. Down to about 34 mg by 11 pm." From ``CaffeineStatus``, in the same words as the decay card. |
| `GetLastCupIntent` | "When's my last cup in \(.applicationName)?" | None | ``ObserveCaffeineCutoffUseCase`` | "Have your usual latte by 2:30 pm to be at 40 mg or less by 10:30 pm." Or that there's no more today. From ``CaffeineCutoff``. |
| `GetCaffeineTodayIntent` | "How much caffeine have I had today in \(.applicationName)?" | None | ``ObserveCaffeineIntakeTodayUseCase`` | "About 192 mg so far today." Whether this intent earns its place is a decision below. |
| `AskHalfLifeIntent` | "Ask \(.applicationName) a question" | `question`, a `String`, which Siri asks for: "What would you like to know?" | ``RespondToInstructionUseCase``, with a ``LanguageModelInstruction`` whose origin is `appIntent` | The model's answer. Its session gets the read-only tools and `logDrink`, so "I just had a cold brew" logs one (<doc:LanguageModel>). |

- **The drink catalog is an `AppEnum`.** `DrinkTypeAppEnum` has one case per ``DrinkType``, with the same raw values, and maps both ways with an exhaustive `switch`, so a new drink that isn't mirrored fails to compile. It's a mirror rather than `extension DrinkType: AppEnum` for the same reason `GeneratedDrinkType` is one: the Domain type stays free of framework conformances, and it's unclear whether App Intents' build-time metadata extraction reads a conformance declared in an extension. Its display names are the composer's names, under the same keys in `Localizable.xcstrings`.
- **The Action Button is one press.** In the Shortcuts app, the user sets up "Log a Drink" with a drink chosen, and assigns it to the Action Button. One press logs it without opening the app, which matches the brief's one-tap logging. The Control Center control (rank 25) can run the same intent later.
- **Answers are honest.** The dialogs say "about", as the screens do, and give amounts in whole milligrams. The model phrases only what its tools report, and says when something isn't available.

## Localization

- **Dialogs** are `LocalizedStringResource`s in `Localizable.xcstrings`, with each formatted value interpolated into one whole sentence (constitution Article VII.2). Amounts and times are formatted with locale-aware APIs (VII.3). `IntentDialogFormat` builds them in Presentation. It can't reuse the Language Model's `LanguageModelFormat`, which is in Data.
- **App Shortcut phrases** can only be localized in a String Catalog named `AppShortcuts.xcstrings`. Article VII.1 names only `Localizable.xcstrings` and `InfoPlist.xcstrings`, so this third catalog needs the owner's decision (see "Decisions to make").
- **The model's answer** is in the user's language, as the Language Model article describes.

## Privacy and logging

- **Nothing leaves the device through Half-Life.** The intents run on the device, and the app makes no network request (constitution Article V.1). What the user says to Siri, and what Siri says back, are handled by Siri under Apple's own privacy terms.
- **The locked device.** An intent's `authenticationPolicy` decides whether it runs while the device is locked: `alwaysAllowed`, `requiresAuthentication`, or `requiresLocalDeviceAuthentication`. Caffeine levels and intake are health data, so anyone holding a locked phone could hear them. The profile is also `NSFileProtectionComplete`, so the bedtime can't be read while the device is locked (<doc:Onboarding>). That leaves the status without its bedtime figure, and the cutoff without an answer at all. The Drink Composer article left this decision to App Intents.
- **Nothing an intent receives or says is logged.** Drinks, amounts, questions, and answers are health data (Article XI.6). A successful intent is a routine health event, so it isn't logged (XI.7). A failure is logged at `error`, with the error's domain and code, and nothing else. Intents are Presentation, so they log through `Logger(for:)` like reducers (XI.3).
- **Donations.** The system records the intents a user runs so that it can suggest them later, on the device. A donated "Log a Drink" carries the drink, which is health data held by the system. Whether to limit donations is an open question.

## Testing

- **Unit tests, in Swift Testing.** Each intent is a plain struct. A test sets its parameters and calls `perform()` inside `withDependencies`, with the use case built on in-memory fake repositories, never live ones. `perform()` returns an opaque `IntentResult`, so its dialog can't be read back. The text is built by `IntentDialogFormat`, which the tests check directly, in the `en_US` locale and New York's time zone. `perform()` stays thin enough that the tests cover it.
- **The shortcuts provider** is tested by reading `appShortcuts`: one shortcut per intent, each phrase containing the app's name.
- **No AppIntentsTesting.** Apple's AppIntentsTesting framework runs intents out of process, the way Siri does. It starts in iOS 27, and the app targets iOS 26.5.
- **No new robot while there's no snippet.** A dialog is Siri's UI, not a screen of the app's, so there's nothing for a robot to find by identifier. A snippet view would be a screen (constitution Articles II.5 and VI.4).

## Out of scope

- **SiriKit's older `INIntent`s.** App Intents replace them.
- **Texting a phone number.** It would need a server and an SMS provider (Article V.1).
- **An entity for logged drinks**, such as "delete my last drink". Delete + undo is rank 20.
- **App schemas** for Apple Intelligence. Whether any of Apple's app schema domains fits a caffeine log hasn't been checked.
- **Widgets and controls.** They're rank 25, and they'll run these intents.

## Decisions to make

| # | Question | AI's recommendation |
|---|----------|---------------------|
| 1 | **Constitution gap.** Article I describes Presentation as reducers and views, and I.3 says "a reducer" reaches business logic through use cases. Intents are presentation that isn't a reducer. | Amend I.3 to name App Intents: they call use cases directly, with no store, and never a repository or data source. |
| 2 | **Constitution gap.** Article VII.1 names two String Catalogs, and App Shortcut phrases need a third, `AppShortcuts.xcstrings`. | Amend VII.1 to name it, for App Shortcut phrases only. |
| 3 | **The locked device.** Which intents may run while the device is locked? | `requiresAuthentication` for all five. Every answer is health data, the bedtime can't be read while the device is locked anyway, and a stranger could log drinks. |
| 4 | **Ask Half-Life without Apple Intelligence.** The owner's rule hides features that use the model while it's unavailable. App Shortcuts are declared when the app is built, so it's likely the shortcut can't be hidden. | While the model is unavailable, Ask Half-Life replies that answers need Apple Intelligence, and names the other shortcuts. |
| 5 | **Today's intake.** The brief asks whether the app protected its single question, *when*. `GetCaffeineTodayIntent` answers *how much*. | Cut it. The status answer already gives the level now, and Ask Half-Life can answer "how much today?" when the model is available. |
| 6 | **The log's answer.** Should logging also say what the drink does to bedtime, as the pre-log cutoff warning (rank 13) will? | Add the bedtime level: "Logged a latte, 2 shots: about 125 mg. You'll have about 51 mg in you at 10:30 pm." That's the app's question, answered where the user is. |
| 7 | **Snippets.** Dialog only, or a result snippet with the decay curve? | Dialog only for now. A snippet is a new screen, with a robot and an audit, and Siri may not show one anyway. |
| 8 | **The SiriKit entitlement.** The app has `com.apple.developer.siri` and `NSSiriUsageDescription`, which SiriKit's older intents need. It's likely that App Intents need neither. | Confirm, and if so remove both (Article V.2: minimum data). |
| 9 | **Enforcement.** Should SwiftLint require `nonisolated` on intents, as it requires `@MainActor` on views? | Yes. It's a one-line custom rule, like the reducer rule. |

## Apple documentation

The framework:

- [App Intents](https://developer.apple.com/documentation/appintents)
- [Getting started with the App Intents framework](https://developer.apple.com/documentation/appintents/getting-started-with-the-app-intents-framework)
- [Creating your first app intent](https://developer.apple.com/documentation/appintents/creating-your-first-app-intent)
- [Adding parameters to an app intent](https://developer.apple.com/documentation/appintents/adding-parameters-to-an-app-intent)
- [Configuring the runtime behavior of your app intents](https://developer.apple.com/documentation/appintents/configuring-the-runtime-behavior-of-your-app-intents): `supportedModes`, foreground and background.

Types this design uses:

- [`AppIntent`](https://developer.apple.com/documentation/appintents/appintent), [`IntentParameter`](https://developer.apple.com/documentation/appintents/intentparameter), [`IntentModes`](https://developer.apple.com/documentation/appintents/intentmodes)
- [`IntentAuthenticationPolicy`](https://developer.apple.com/documentation/appintents/intentauthenticationpolicy): running while the device is locked.
- [`IntentResult`](https://developer.apple.com/documentation/appintents/intentresult), [`ProvidesDialog`](https://developer.apple.com/documentation/appintents/providesdialog), [`IntentDialog`](https://developer.apple.com/documentation/appintents/intentdialog)
- [`AppEnum`](https://developer.apple.com/documentation/appintents/appenum): a parameter with a fixed set of values.
- [`AppDependencyManager`](https://developer.apple.com/documentation/appintents/appdependencymanager) and [`AppDependency`](https://developer.apple.com/documentation/appintents/appdependency): Apple's dependency registry, rejected in favour of swift-dependencies.

Siri, Shortcuts, and the Action Button:

- [App Shortcuts](https://developer.apple.com/documentation/appintents/app-shortcuts), [`AppShortcutsProvider`](https://developer.apple.com/documentation/appintents/appshortcutsprovider), [`AppShortcut`](https://developer.apple.com/documentation/appintents/appshortcut), [`AppShortcutPhrase`](https://developer.apple.com/documentation/appintents/appshortcutphrase)
- [`SiriTipView`](https://developer.apple.com/documentation/appintents/siritipview) and [`ShortcutsLink`](https://developer.apple.com/documentation/appintents/shortcutslink): in-app hints, not part of this design.
- [Hardware interactions](https://developer.apple.com/documentation/appintents/hardware-interactions): the Action Button.
- [Apple Intelligence and Siri AI](https://developer.apple.com/documentation/appintents/apple-intelligence-and-siri-ai) and [App schema domains](https://developer.apple.com/documentation/appintents/app-schema-domains)
- [Donating your app's data and actions to the system](https://developer.apple.com/documentation/appintents/donating-your-apps-data-and-actions-to-the-system)

Snippets, if the owner chooses one:

- [Displaying static and interactive snippets](https://developer.apple.com/documentation/appintents/displaying-static-and-interactive-snippets), [`ShowsSnippetView`](https://developer.apple.com/documentation/appintents/showssnippetview), [`SnippetIntent`](https://developer.apple.com/documentation/appintents/snippetintent)

Testing:

- [Verifying your App Intents implementation](https://developer.apple.com/documentation/appintents/verifying-your-app-intents-implementation)
- [AppIntentsTesting](https://developer.apple.com/documentation/appintentstesting): iOS 27 and later, so not yet usable here.

Videos:

- [Get to know App Intents](https://developer.apple.com/videos/play/wwdc2025/244/), WWDC25
- [Explore new advances in App Intents](https://developer.apple.com/videos/play/wwdc2025/275/), WWDC25: interactive snippets and `supportedModes`.

- [Foundation Models](https://developer.apple.com/documentation/foundationmodels), and <doc:LanguageModel> for how Half-Life uses it.

### 23:22:22 · Claude

I've written the App Intents design as a new DocC article, `Half-Life/Documentation.docc/AppIntents.md`, and listed it under Features. It opens with the architecture diagram, and ends with a list of Apple's documentation and the WWDC25 App Intents sessions. Nothing is built yet.

**The architecture**
- **Where the intents live:** `Half-Life/AppIntents/`, in the Presentation layer. They run in the app's own process, not an extension, so they use the same app-wide repositories as the screens. An extension would be a second process writing to the same SwiftData store.
- **Five intents:** log a drink, the caffeine level now and at bedtime, the last cup, today's intake, and "Ask Half-Life". The last one passes the user's words to `RespondToInstructionUseCase` and covers the brief's "text the app" bonus.
- **No new business logic:** every intent calls a use case that already exists. Questions take the first value from the existing `Observe…` use cases, so Siri's numbers can't disagree with the Today screen.

**Findings from the iOS 26.5 SDK and Apple's docs**
- **A naming trap:** inside an intent, a bare `@Dependency` resolves to Apple's `AppDependency`, not swift-dependencies'. Intents have to write `@Dependencies.Dependency(\.logDrink)`. Whether that compiles through TCA's re-export will only be known at the first build.
- **AppIntentsTesting requires iOS 27**, so it's not available for our iOS 26.5 target. Intents will be unit-tested by calling `perform()` against fake repositories, with the dialog text built by a separate function that the tests check directly.

**Decisions I need from you**
The article lists nine, each with my recommendation. Two are gaps in the constitution:
1. **Article I.3** only covers reducers, and intents are presentation code that isn't a reducer. I recommend amending I.3 to allow intents to call use cases directly.
2. **Article VII.1** names two String Catalogs, but App Shortcut phrases need a third, `AppShortcuts.xcstrings`. I recommend naming it there, for the phrases only.

The other seven are:
- **Locked device:** I recommend requiring authentication for all five intents, because every answer is health data. The profile can't be read while the phone is locked anyway.
- **Ask Half-Life without Apple Intelligence:** a shortcut probably can't be hidden at runtime. I recommend replying that answers need Apple Intelligence.
- **Today's intake:** I recommend cutting this intent, since it answers "how much" rather than the app's question, "when".
- **The log's reply:** I recommend adding the caffeine level the drink leaves at bedtime.
- **Snippets:** I recommend Siri's spoken or written reply only for now, with no custom view.
- **The Siri entitlement:** I recommend removing it and its purpose string if App Intents don't need them.
- **SwiftLint:** I recommend a rule requiring intents to be marked `nonisolated`.

A few statements in the design come from forum and community sources, not Apple's own pages. The article flags them: that phrases must include the app's name, that phrases are localized through `AppShortcuts.xcstrings`, and that the Siri entitlement isn't needed.

`docbuild` succeeded with no warnings from our catalog. No code changed, so I ran no tests. The `ai_log.md` entry is appended. Nothing is committed.

Sources:
- [SnippetIntent](https://developer.apple.com/documentation/appintents/snippetintent)
- [Explore new advances in App Intents (WWDC25)](https://developer.apple.com/videos/play/wwdc2025/275/)
- [Get to know App Intents (WWDC25)](https://developer.apple.com/videos/play/wwdc2025/244/)
- [Localizing App Shortcuts with App Intents (Localazy)](https://localazy.com/blog/localizing-app-shortcuts-with-app-intents)
- [Localizing App Shortcuts with App Intents (Arnaud Joubay)](https://sowenjub.me/writes/localizing-app-shortcuts-with-app-intents/)
- [Apple Developer Forums: AppShortcuts.xcstrings](https://developer.apple.com/forums/thread/797784)

Yes, I'm the App Intents session (6aabc47d). The intent is LogDrinkIntent. Its shape and answers to your six points follow.

Status: design only, in Half-Life/Documentation.docc/AppIntents.md, not built and not committed. The owner has 10 open decisions, and if any of them changes a name or parameter I'll tell you.

1. Shape. `nonisolated struct LogDrinkIntent: AppIntent`, in Half-Life/AppIntents/LogDrinkIntent.swift.
   - `@Parameter var drink: DrinkTypeAppEnum`: required. It's an AppEnum mirror of DrinkType in Half-Life/AppIntents/DrinkTypeAppEnum.swift, with the same raw values and an exhaustive mapping in both directions.
   - `@Parameter var quantity: Int?`: optional, and nil means the drink's defaultQuantity. At least 1, which DrinkLogRule checks.
   - secondsAgo is always 0.
   - I've added `init(drink: DrinkTypeAppEnum, quantity: Int)` beside `init()`, for your `Button(intent:)`. Your AppIntentConfiguration can use DrinkTypeAppEnum directly.

2. Agreed, and it breaks an assumption in my design. I kept every intent in the app target so there's one process: an extension means a second process, with its own repositories over the same store, so two writers whose signals don't cross. I've added this to the article as decision 10 for the owner.
   - A pitfall for you, which I verified in the iOS 26.5 SDK: inside any AppIntent, a bare `@Dependency` resolves to `AppIntents.AppDependency`, through a typealias on `_SupportsAppDependencies`. Write `@Dependencies.Dependency(\.logDrink)`. The extension's live DependencyKey values will also build a second DrinkLogRepository instance.
   - Please send me what Apple's docs say about which process runs an intent compiled into both targets, and especially whether Siri's runs of LogDrinkIntent would then land in the extension too. I couldn't verify either.

3. Logging a drink needs only the drink store. LogDrinkUseCase uses CurrentTimeRepository and DrinkLogRepository, which use DrinkLogDataSource and the clock. It never reads Profile.json or HalfLifeEstimate.json, so the widget's log doesn't force the protection change on its own.
   - For Siri, I'm recommending `authenticationPolicy = .requiresAuthentication` on all five intents, because every answer is health data. That avoids reading on a locked phone, so my side doesn't need the weaker class.
   - One catch is my decision 6, which would add the bedtime level to the log's reply. That reads CaffeineDecayRepository, and so the profile. If the owner picks it, the intent drops that line when the profile can't be read.
   - I don't know whether authenticationPolicy applies to a widget button on the Lock Screen. Worth checking if the log goes on a Lock Screen widget.
   - Weakening those two files needs the reason documented in the catalog (Article V.4).

4. Agreed. `supportedModes = .background`. `openAppWhenRun` is deprecated in iOS 26's SDK anyway.

5. Not handling it, so it's yours. If Siri's runs also land in the extension, your remote-change listening covers them.

6. Yours too. I suggest triggering the reload from the drink log's change, on the data source or repository side, not from the intents. Then the composer, one-tap, Siri, delete, and the demo history all trigger it through one hook. LogDrinkIntent calls only LogDrinkUseCase.

Files I'm touching: AppIntents.md, one line in Documentation.md's topics, and ai_log.md. No data sources and no project.pbxproj.

### 23:26:39 · Claude

The widgets session, half-life-3c, asked how its one-tap widget should call our log-drink intent, and that exposed a problem in my design. I'd put every intent inside the app so that only one process ever writes the drink store. A widget button apparently runs its intent in the widget's own process instead, which would mean two processes writing the same store. half-life-3c is still confirming that in Apple's documentation.

I've added this to `AppIntents.md` as decision 10. My recommendation is to accept it, with the fixes half-life-3c proposed:
- **Shared container:** the drink store moves to an App Group container that both the app and the widget can reach.
- **Keeping the app current:** the drink log data source listens for changes the other process makes, so the app sees drinks the widget logs.
- **Keeping the widget small:** logging a drink needs only the drink store, so the widget never reads `Profile.json` or `HalfLifeEstimate.json`.

**What I told half-life-3c**
- **The intent:** `LogDrinkIntent`, with a required drink parameter, an optional quantity, and a new `init(drink:quantity:)` so a widget button can build it. I've added that initializer to the design. I also warned it about the `@Dependency` naming trap inside intents.
- **What it owns:** keeping the app up to date when another process logs a drink, and refreshing the widgets after each log. I suggested triggering the refresh from the drink log itself rather than from the intents, so every way of logging or deleting updates the widgets.
- **Locked phone:** half-life-3c wants to weaken the file protection on `Profile.json` and `HalfLifeEstimate.json` so they can be read while the phone is locked. Siri doesn't need that if you take my recommendation (decision 3) to require unlocking for all five intents. That change is still pending your approval.

**Still open**
- **Where Siri's runs happen:** I don't know whether Siri's runs of the intent would also move to the widget's process once it's built into both. I've asked half-life-3c to share what Apple's documentation says.
- **Your decisions:** the ten in the article.

The rerun of `docbuild` succeeded. My article adds no warnings. Two new warnings come from another session's unfinished cutoff-reminder files, which link to a `CutoffReminderFeature` that doesn't exist yet. My `ai_log.md` entry covers the exchange. Nothing is committed.

I haven't verified either.

3. The compilation condition has to cover the dependency property as well as `perform()`. `@Dependencies.Dependency(\.logDrink)` needs both the Dependencies module and the app's `logDrink` key. So either the widget links Dependencies and the key registration is left out of it, or the property sits inside the condition too.

4. The snapshot is health data: caffeine levels derived from the drink log. It needs a row in the Architecture article's "Data and privacy" table, and its own protection class. If Lock Screen widgets read it, anything weaker than Complete is a documented exception under Article V.4. No health values from it can be logged (XI.6).

Tell me when the owner decides and I'll update decision 10 then.

### 23:28:09 · Claude

The widget question now has a better answer than decision 10 in my article. I've left the article as it is, because half-life-3c asked me to wait for your decision.

**What half-life-3c found in Apple's documentation**
By default, a widget's intent runs in the widget's own process. It runs in the app's process instead if the intent adopts one of a few protocols, such as `LiveActivityIntent`.

**What it's recommending to you** (not decided)
- **One writer:** `LogDrinkIntent` adopts `LiveActivityIntent`, so the app stays the only process that writes the drink store. That keeps my original single-process design.
- **Widget data:** the widgets read a snapshot the app writes to a shared App Group container.
- **Nothing weakened:** no file protection changes, and no need to watch for another process's writes.

If you accept it, I'll update decision 10 to match.

**My four concerns, which I've sent to half-life-3c**
- **Intended use:** `LiveActivityIntent` is meant for Live Activities, and we'd be adopting it only to get the app's process. I haven't checked whether it needs extra Info.plist setup or clashes with running in the background.
- **Shortcut list:** only the log intent and the drink list should go into the widget target. The list of Siri shortcuts stays in the app, so they aren't declared twice.
- **Widget build:** the code that keeps the intent from running in the widget also has to cover the line that fetches the log use case, not just the method that runs the intent.
- **Privacy:** the snapshot holds caffeine levels, which are health data. It needs a row in the Architecture article's "Data and privacy" table and a documented file protection class. None of its values can be logged.

My `ai_log.md` entry records both exchanges and now ends at 23:27. Nothing is committed.

**1. The nine open decisions** (the table at the end of `AppIntents.md`)

These change the code, so they're worth settling first:
- **#1, constitution I.3:** intents are presentation code without a reducer, which Article I doesn't cover. half-life-3c is writing an amendment for the same gap in widgets, so one amendment could cover both. I've told half-life-3c about the overlap.
- **#2, constitution VII.1:** Siri's shortcut phrases need a third String Catalog, `AppShortcuts.xcstrings`.
- **#3, locked phone:** I recommend requiring the phone to be unlocked for all five intents.

These change what the app says or includes:
- **#4:** what "Ask Half-Life" says when Apple Intelligence is unavailable.
- **#5:** whether to cut the today's-intake intent. I recommend cutting it, because it answers "how much" rather than the app's question, "when".
- **#6:** whether the log's reply also gives the caffeine left at bedtime.
- **#7:** snippets, meaning a custom view in Siri's reply. I recommend the spoken or written reply only.
- **#8:** removing the Siri entitlement and its purpose string.
- **#9:** a SwiftLint rule requiring intents to be marked `nonisolated`.

**2. Choices I made without asking**
- **Siri logs only "now".** "Log a drink" can't record one from earlier. "Ask Half-Life" can, because its log tool accepts up to 12 hours ago, so the two paths behave differently. Backdating drinks is rank 17 on the roadmap.
- **Quantity has no limit through Siri**, while the language model's log tool caps it at 10.
- **The five intents, their names, and the English phrases** are my drafts.
- **The replies say "about" and give whole milligrams**, matching the Today screen.
- **Architecture:** questions reuse the existing `Observe…` use cases and take their first value, so there are no new use cases. Dependencies come from swift-dependencies rather than Apple's `AppDependencyManager`. The drink list is a separate `DrinkTypeAppEnum` rather than a conformance added to `DrinkType`.

**3. A privacy question that's not in the table**
The system records each intent a user runs so it can suggest it later, on the device. A recorded "Log a Drink" includes the drink, which is health data. Should we limit that?

**4. Facts I haven't verified against Apple's own pages**
These came from forums and community posts, or from memory:
- **Phrases:** every shortcut phrase must include the app's name, and phrases are translated only through `AppShortcuts.xcstrings`.
- **Hiding:** a shortcut can't be hidden while the app runs, which is the basis for #4.
- **Siri entitlement:** App Intents don't need it, which is the basis for #8.
- **`AppEnum` in an extension:** the build step that reads intents may not see an `AppEnum` conformance declared in an extension, which is why I chose the separate `DrinkTypeAppEnum`.
- **Build:** whether `@Dependencies.Dependency` compiles through TCA's re-export will only be known at the first build.
- **`LiveActivityIntent`:** whether it needs extra Info.plist setup, or clashes with running in the background. half-life-3c is testing this before anything is built.

**5. What I left out**
Check you agree with these:
- **Deleting by voice:** there's no "delete my last drink", because delete and undo is rank 20.
- **Apple Intelligence schemas:** not adopted. I haven't checked whether any of Apple's categories fits a caffeine log.
- **Texting a phone number:** it would need a server, which the constitution rules out.

I haven't rerun `docbuild` since these last article edits. They add no symbol links, so the risk of a new warning is low. The log entry is updated, and nothing is committed.

**23:41:55 · Tool result**

```
1	# Half-Life Constitution
2	
3	> **Status: DRAFT** — starter version for review. Edit, remove, or add principles, then change the status to **Ratified** with the date.
4	
5	This document defines the foundational principles every change to Half-Life must follow. It overrides convenience, existing code patterns, and any other project guidance (including `CLAUDE.md`). If a change can't comply with it, the change is wrong, or this document needs a deliberate amendment. It is never quietly worked around.
6	
7	## Article I: Architecture
8	
9	The app combines **The Composable Architecture (TCA)** ([pointfreeco/swift-composable-architecture](https://github.com/pointfreeco/swift-composable-architecture)) with **Clean Architecture**. It has three layers.
10	
11	| Layer | Contains | May depend on |
12	|-------|----------|---------------|
13	| **Presentation** | TCA features: reducers and their views | Domain (use cases and entities) |
14	| **Domain** | Entities, business rules, use cases, and repository protocols | Nothing (Swift and Foundation only) |
15	| **Data** | Repository implementations and data sources (HealthKit, SwiftData, …) | Domain (to implement its protocols) |
16	
17	Dependencies point inward, toward Domain. Domain never imports SwiftUI, ComposableArchitecture, HealthKit, or SwiftData. Nothing depends on Presentation.
18	
19	### Presentation: TCA features
20	
21	1. **Features are reducers.** Each feature is a `@Reducer` with an `@ObservableState` `State`, an `Action` enum, and a `body`. Presentation logic lives in reducers, never in views.
22	2. **Views are thin.** A view renders from a `StoreOf<Feature>` and sends actions. It holds no business state of its own (`@State` only for purely visual, ephemeral concerns).
23	3. **Actions call use cases.** A reducer reaches business logic and data only through use cases, injected with `@Dependency`, and calls them from `Effect`s. It never touches a repository or data source directly.
24	4. **Repository data is reduced into State.** A feature subscribes to repository data through an `Observe…` use case in a `.run` effect. The feature sends one action per emitted value (e.g. `.entriesUpdated([CaffeineEntry])`), and the reducer reduces that value into `State`. The subscription is tied to the feature's lifetime: the view starts it from `.task { await store.send(.task).finish() }`, or it is cancelled by ID when the feature is dismissed.
25	5. **One-way data flow.** Commands flow action → use case → repository. Updates flow repository → `Observe…` use case → action → reducer → `State`. A reducer does not optimistically write repository-owned data into `State`; it waits for the repository to emit. Any documented exception must be recorded in the DocC catalog.
26	6. **Composition and navigation.** Features compose with `Scope`, `.ifLet`, and `.forEach`. Navigation is state-driven using `@Presents` / `PresentationAction` and `StackState` / `StackAction`. These rules govern the app's own screens. System UI that a framework provides, such as MessageUI's Mail composer or HealthKit's authorization sheet, is presented by the data source that wraps the framework (I.14). The feature learns the outcome through the repository, like any other data.
27	
28	### Domain: use cases, entities, and business rules
29	
30	7. **Use cases are single-purpose.** Each use case performs exactly one business operation. It conforms to the Domain's `UseCase` protocol and exposes the operation as `execute(_:)`, which takes one `Input` and returns one `Output` (`Void` when there's none). It is named for that operation: `LogCaffeineIntakeUseCase`, `ObserveCaffeineEntriesUseCase`.
31	8. **Use cases are lifetime-scoped.** A use case lives only as long as the feature that owns it. It holds no state of its own beyond references to the repositories it uses; long-lived state belongs in repositories, and per-screen state belongs in feature `State`. Use cases never cache data between calls.
32	9. **Use cases depend on repository protocols**, which Domain defines. They never depend on concrete implementations.
33	10. **Entities and business rules.** Entities are plain `Sendable`, `Equatable` value types with no framework dependencies. Business rules are stateless Domain types that calculate from entities, such as the caffeine decay curve. Repositories execute business rules to derive the data they publish. A business rule holds no state and never touches a repository or data source.
34	
35	### Data: repositories and data sources
36	
37	11. **Repositories hold state.** A repository is the single source of truth for its data. It is app-scoped, created once, and outlives every feature. It holds the current data in memory and persists it through data sources.
38	12. **Repositories publish changes.** Every repository exposes its data as an `AsyncStream` that emits the current value immediately on subscription and then every change.
39	13. **Repositories are concurrency-safe and stay off the main actor.** Each is its own `actor`, or is isolated to a single global actor other than the main actor (Article IV.1).
40	14. **Data sources wrap frameworks.** Data sources are the only code that touches HealthKit, SwiftData, the file system, or other system frameworks. Repositories talk to data sources, never to frameworks directly.
```
